High-Dimensional Vector Autoregression with Common Response and Predictor Factors
Di Wang, Xiaoyu Zhang, Guodong Li, Ruey S. Tsay, School of Mathematical Sciences, Shanghai Jiao Tong University

Abstract
The reduced-rank vector autoregressive (VAR) model can be interpreted as a supervised factor model, where two factor modelings are simultaneously applied to response and predictor spaces. This article introduces a new model, called vector autoregression with common response and predictor factors, to explore further the common structure between the response and predictors in the VAR framework. The new model can provide better physical interpretations and improve estimation efficiency. In conjunction with the tensor operation, the model can easily be extended to any finite-order VAR model. A regularization-based method is considered for the high-dimensional estimation with the gradient descent algorithm, and its computational and statistical convergence guarantees are established. For data with pervasive cross-sectional dependence, a transformation for responses is developed to alleviate the diverging eigenvalue effect. Moreover, we consider additional sparsity structure in factor loading for the case of ultra-high dimension. Simulation experiments confirm our theoretical findings and a macroeconomic application showcases the appealing properties of the proposed model in structural analysis and forecasting.



Keywords
: Factor model, High-dimensional time series, Gradient descent, Matrix factorization, Tensor decomposition

1
Introduction

Due to recent developments in information technologies, high-dimensional data, especially time-dependent data, have been routinely collected from a wide range of scientific areas, including economics, finance, neuroscience, and meteorology, among others
(
Gorrostieta et al., 2012
;
Hallin and Lippi, 2013
;
Dowell and Pinson, 2016
)
.
The well-developed statistical methodology for fixed-dimensional data may not be directly applicable to high-dimensional cases, and large-scale data sets often also require scalable and efficient computational algorithms. As a result, it becomes an emerging area of research to develop new statistical methodology and theoretically justified algorithms to analyze the high dimensional data
(
Wainwright, 2019
;
Chi et al., 2019
)
. In addition, more efforts are needed for high-dimensional time series data due to its complex dynamic dependency; see
Peña and Tsay, 2021
.

The vector autoregressive (VAR) model, arguably the most widely used model in
multivariate time series applications, has been a primary workhorse for analyzing serially dependent data.
Consider a VAR(1) model for a
p
-dimensional mean-zero time series
\{\mathbf{y}_{t}\}
,

\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},~~1\leq t\leq T,

(1.1)

where
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is a white noise process with mean zero and the covariance matrix
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
, and
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
is the parameter matrix providing a straightforward characterization of the interactions between the response
\mathbf{y}_{t}
and predictor
\mathbf{y}_{t-1}
; see
Tsay, 2014
.
Note that the number of parameters in
\mathbf{A}
increases quadratically with the dimension
p
, making it difficult to apply VAR models to high-dimensional data.
To overcome it, a commonly used solution is to assume sparsity in parameter matrices, and many sparsity-imposing or inducing methods can then be employed for estimation and variable selection, including
L_{1}
regularization
(
Basu and Michailidis, 2015
;
Han et al., 2015
)
and linear restrictions
(
Guo et al., 2016
;
Wang and Tsay, 2023
)
.

Despite its popularity in the literature, there are two concerns about sparse VAR modeling. First, the general sparsity structure cannot guarantee the spectral radius condition for stationarity, so a sparse estimate may result in a non-stationary VAR model.
Second, for financial and economic time series, one often observes strong dependence among the
p
scalar series, which often is investigated via factor models
by assuming that the
p
variables can be decomposed into two parts, factors and errors.
In the vast literature of econometrics and statistics, there are two classes of factor models under various assumptions on factors and errors. The first class assumes that factors are common cross-sectionally and allows for serially dependent idiosyncratic errors
(
Stock and Watson, 2002
;
Bai and Ng, 2002
;
Bai and Ng, 2008
)
; while the second class assumes that the dynamic structures along the temporal direction are summarized in the factors, and the errors are temporally uncorrelated
(
Peña and Box, 1987
;
Lam and Yao, 2012
;
Gao and Tsay, 2022
)
.

Another solution for handling high dimensionality is to impose some low-rank structure on the parameter matrices of VAR models, and it leads to the reduced-rank model
(
Velu and Reinsel, 2013
)
or the multilinear low-rank model
(
Wang et al., 2022
)
.
This method can circumvent the two concerns mentioned above, and especially for financial and economic data, the fitted models can be interpreted from the perspective of the second class of factor models.
Specifically, assume that
\mathbf{A}
is of rank
r
with
r\ll p
and, hence, it admits a singular value decomposition (SVD)
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{V}^{\top}
, where
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{V}
are
p
-by-
r
orthonormal matrices.
Accordingly, model (
1.1
) can then be rewritten as

\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}+\mathbf{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},

(1.2)

and this motivates us to interpret
\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}
and
\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}
as
r
-dimensional response and predictor factors, respectively, where
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{V}
are the factor loading matrices.
The factors defined here can summarize the temporal dynamics in responses and predictors, and should be understood in the sense of the second class of factor models, in which the factors capture all dynamic dependency in the data.
Along this line, we reformulate the VAR model with a low-rank parameter matrix into a form of supervised factor modeling in Section
2.1
.
Specifically, it is equivalent to simultaneously conducting two factor modelings for the
p
financial or economic variables in a market, where the latent response factors
\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}
can summarize the whole market as in the traditional factor modeling, while the latent predictor factors
\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}
are the driving forces of the market; see Section
2
for more details.

Although the factor model cannot directly be used for forecasting, a common practice is to apply a low-dimensional model to the factor processes, and then use predictions of the factors and the loading matrices to obtain forecasts of the high-dimensional time series
(
Lam and Yao, 2012
;
Gao and Tsay, 2022
)
.
For example, for a
p
-dimensional time series
\mathbf{y}_{t}
, consider the factor model in
Gao and Tsay, 2022
, which can be written as
\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{f}_{t}+\mathbf{\Gamma}\mathbf{e}_{t}
with
\mathbf{f}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
being a low-dimensional factor process and
\mathbf{e}_{t}
a
(p-r)
-dimensional white noise, and the loading matrix
\textbf{[}\mathbf{\Lambda}~\mathbf{\Gamma}\textbf{]}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}

being orthonormal.
Assuming a VAR(1) model for
\mathbf{f}_{t}
, say
\mathbf{f}_{t}=\mathbf{B}\mathbf{f}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}
, it can be shown that
\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}
follows a VAR(1) process

\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}.

(1.3)

In comparison with model (
1.2
), the spaces spanned by the response and predictor factors in model (
1.3
) are identical; see Section
2.1
for more discussions on its relationship to VAR models.
As shown by the empirical example in Section
8
, the setting of factor models may be too restrictive, while the spaces spanned by
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{V}
from VAR models in (
1.2
) may be overlapped, i.e., there may exist common factors in responses and predictors.
The first main contribution of this article is to propose a VAR model with common response and predictor factors in Section
2.2
, where dynamic dependence in time series is summarized into three types of factors: response-specific, predictor-specific, and common factors.
This enables a better physical interpretation and facilitates the development of more efficient estimation.

We then consider the high-dimensional estimation method and algorithm. A form of matrix or tensor decomposition is considered for the proposed model. However, as the decomposition is not unique and the optimization problem is non-convex, it is challenging to derive computational and statistical guarantees.
To this end, the second contribution of this article is to develop a complete modeling procedure for estimation and parameter selection in Section
3
and further to provide theoretical justifications for both computational and statistical convergence in Section
4
. Specifically, a regularized estimation framework is proposed for high-dimensional VAR models with common response and predictor factors, and a scalable and efficient gradient descent algorithm with spectral initialization is developed accordingly.
From the computational and statistical convergence analysis, the proposed procedure can effectively and efficiently achieve a statistically optimal rate for estimation errors. Moreover, a data-driven procedure is suggested to determine the numbers of common and specific factors, and its theoretical justifications are also established.

To adequately address the strong cross-sectional dependence of time series data in the many real applications, in Section
5
, we further investigate the case where the largest eigenvalue of
\text{var}(\mathbf{y}_{t})
may diverge to infinity as
p
increases. The third contribution of this article is to provide the first solution to deal with the diverging eigenvalue effect, or pervasive cross-sectional dependency, in high-dimensional VAR estimation.
Additionally, in Section
6
, for the case of
p\gg T
, we consider an additional sparsity structure on the factor loading matrices to improve estimation efficiency and to perform variable selection.
Finally, some simulation results and an empirical example are presented in Sections
7
and
8
, respectively. Section
9
gives a short conclusion with discussion. All technical proofs, codes, data, and additional simulation results are given in appendices.

This work is also related to the vast literature on Bayesian VAR models.
Bańbura et al., 2010
studied the shrinkage prior for large Bayesian VAR models, and
Koop, 2013
applied it to the macroeconomic data of medium and large sizes. Bayesian variable selection method for VAR processes was first proposed by
Korobilis, 2013
.
Ghosh et al., 2019
and
Ghosh et al., 2021
studied posterior estimation consistency and strong variable selection consistency of large Bayesian VAR models, respectively.

Throughout this article, we denote vectors by boldface lower case letters, e.g.,
\mathbf{v}
, matrices by boldface capital letters, e.g.,
\mathbf{M}
, and third-order tensors by Euler script letters, e.g.,
\mathscr{T}
. For any vector
\mathbf{v}
, denote by
\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}
its Euclidean norm. For any matrix
\mathbf{M}
, denote by
\mathbf{M}^{\top}
,
\|\mathbf{M}\|_{\text{F}}
,
\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M})
,
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{M})
, and
\mathcal{M}^{\perp}(\mathbf{M})
its transpose, Frobenius norm,
i
-th largest singular value, column space, and orthogonal complement of column space, respectively. For a symmetric matrix
\mathbf{M}
, denote by
\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{M})
and
\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{M})
its largest and smallest eigenvalue, respectively. For two matrices
\mathbf{M}_{1}
and
\mathbf{M}_{2}
, denote by
\textbf{[}\mathbf{M}_{1}~\mathbf{M}_{2}\textbf{]}
and
\langle\mathbf{M}_{1},\mathbf{M}_{2}\rangle
their column-wise matrix concatenation and inner product, respectively. For positive integers
p\geq q
, denote the set of orthonormal matrices by
\mathbb{O}^{p\times q}:=\{\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}:\mathbf{M}^{\top}\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{I}_{q}\}
. For a third-order tensor
\mathscr{T}
, denote by
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{T}$}\|_{\textup{F}}
the Frobenius norm and by
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{T}$}_{(i)}
its mode-
i
matricization, for
1\leq i\leq 3
. For a tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{T}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times\dots\times p_{i}\times\dots\times p_{d}}
and matrix
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times p_{i}}
, denote by
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{T}$}\times_{i}\mathbf{M}
the mode-
i
tensor-matrix multiplication, for
1\leq i\leq d
.
Let
C
denote a generic positive constant. For two real-valued sequences
x_{k}
and
y_{k}
,
x_{k}\gtrsim y_{k}
if there exists a
C>0
such that
x_{k}\geq Cy_{k}
for all
k
. In addition, we write
x_{k}\asymp y_{k}
if
x_{k}\gtrsim y_{k}
and
y_{k}\gtrsim x_{k}
.
Some preliminaries of tensor notation and tensor algebra are presented in Appendix
D
.

2
VAR with Common Response and Predictor Factors

2.1
Relationship between reduced-rank VAR and factor models

Consider the VAR(1) model in (
1.1
). Assume that the parameter matrix
\mathbf{A}
has a low rank
r
, which is much smaller than
p
, and admits the SVD
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{V}^{\top}
, where
\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times r}
are orthonormal matrices, and
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
is a diagonal matrix.
As a result, the reduced-rank VAR model can be formulated into

\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{or}\hskip 14.22636pt\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}+\mathbf{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},

(2.1)

where
\{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\}
are
i.i.d.
with mean zero and finite variance matrix; see
Velu and Reinsel, 2013
.
Note that the singular vectors
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{V}
are not unique, as sign switches and column exchanges can be applied. Also, when some of the singular values are identical, their corresponding singular vectors are also not unique. However, the column spaces
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{U})
and
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{V})
, as well as the corresponding subspace projectors
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}
and
\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^{\top}
, can be uniquely defined.
In fact,
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{U})
and
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{V})
are the column and row spaces of
\mathbf{A}
, respectively.

From model (
2.1
), we can interpret
\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}
and
\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}
, respectively, as the response and predictor factors, which correspond to two different factor modelings.
On one hand, for dimension reduction on the response factor space,
\mathbf{y}_{t}
can be projected onto
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{U})
and
\mathcal{M}^{\perp}(\mathbf{U})
, where these two parts can be verified to have completely different dynamic structures,

\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}=(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{V}^{\top})(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1})+\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathbf{y}_{t}=(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}.

(2.2)

All information of
\mathbf{y}_{t}
related to temporal dynamic structures is collected into
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{U})
, and the projection of
\mathbf{y}_{t}
onto
\mathcal{M}^{\perp}(\mathbf{U})
is serially uncorrelated.
In fact, model (
2.1
) can also simply be rewritten as
\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{f}_{t}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
with
\mathbf{f}_{t}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}
. Consequently, the time series generated by model (
2.1
) admits a form of static factor models, and the corresponding factor space is exactly the response factor space
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{U})
.

On the other hand, if we project
\mathbf{y}_{t-1}
onto
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{V})
and
\mathcal{M}^{\perp}(\mathbf{V})
, it holds that

\text{cov}(\mathbf{y}_{t},\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1})=(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{V}^{\top})\text{var}(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1})\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{cov}(\mathbf{y}_{t},(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^{\top})\mathbf{y}_{t-1})=\mathbf{0}.

All information of
\mathbf{y}_{t-1}
that can contribute to predicting
\mathbf{y}_{t}
is summarized into the space
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{V})
, and the predictor factors
\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}
contain all driving forces of the market.
Following the existing work considering dynamically dependent factors and white noise errors
(
Lam and Yao, 2012
)
, we may treat model (
2.1
) as a supervised factor model, where two different factor modelings are conducted simultaneously and the dynamic dependence of time series is driven by these two types of factors.

Factor modeling, with dynamically dependent factors and white noise errors, is another method to forecast high-dimensional time series in the statistics literature; see
Lam et al., 2011
,
Lam and Yao, 2012
,
Gao and Tsay, 2022
, among others.
Specifically, consider the factor model in
Gao and Tsay, 2022
, and assume that
\mathbf{y}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
has a latent structure of

\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{f}_{t}+\mathbf{\Gamma}\mathbf{e}_{t}=\textbf{[}\mathbf{\Lambda}~\mathbf{\Gamma}\textbf{]}\textbf{[}\mathbf{f}_{t}^{\top}~\mathbf{e}_{t}^{\top}\textbf{]}^{\top},~~1\leq t\leq T

(2.3)

where
\mathbf{f}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
is a dynamic factor,
\mathbf{e}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{p-r}
is a white noise, and
\mathbf{\Lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times r}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times(p-r)}
are full-rank loading matrices for factors and white noise components, respectively.
For the sake of identification,
\mathbf{\Lambda}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}
are assumed to be orthonormal, i.e.,
\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top}\mathbf{\Lambda}=\mathbf{I}_{r}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\top}\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{I}_{p-r}
, and
\textbf{[}\mathbf{\Lambda}~\mathbf{\Gamma}\textbf{]}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
is of full rank such that
\text{var}(\mathbf{y}_{t})
is nonsingular.
Furthermore, suppose that the factors in (
2.3
) follow a VAR model,

\mathbf{f}_{t}=\mathbf{B}\mathbf{f}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}
,
where
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
is a coefficient matrix, and
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
is a white noise uncorrelated with
\{\mathbf{e}_{t}\}
in all leads and lags.

Denote
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t}=\mathbf{\Lambda}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}+\mathbf{\Gamma}\mathbf{e}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, which is serially uncorrelated.
Note that
(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top})\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t}=(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top})\mathbf{\Gamma}\mathbf{e}_{t}
and, for the projection of
\mathbf{y}_{t}
onto
\mathcal{M}^{\perp}(\mathbf{\Lambda})
, it holds that

(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top})\mathbf{y}_{t}=(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top})\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t}
, which is also serially uncorrelated.
The projection of
\mathbf{y}_{t}
onto
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{\Lambda})
follows

\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}=(\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top})(\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1})+\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t}-\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top}\textbf{[}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top}\mathbf{\Gamma}~\mathbf{0}_{p\times r}\textbf{]}\textbf{[}\mathbf{\Gamma}~\mathbf{\Lambda}\textbf{]}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t-1},

(2.4)

i.e. a form of vector autoregressive and moving average (VARMA) models
(
Tsay, 2014
)
.
Moreover, when
\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top}\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{0}_{r\times(p-r)}
, it reduces to a VAR(1) process.
In comparison with (
2.2
), the response and predictor spaces in (
2.4
) are identical, and this may be too restrictive; see Section
8
for empirical evidence.

We finally consider model (
2.1
) with the same response and predictor spaces, i.e.,
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{U})=\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{V})
. There exists an orthogonal matrix
\mathbf{O}\in\mathbb{O}^{r\times r}
such that
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{O}=\mathbf{V}
. As a result, model (
2.1
) can be rewritten as
\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
, where
\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{O}^{\top}
,

\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}=(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{U}^{\top})(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1})+\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\quad\text{and}\quad(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\mathbf{y}_{t}=(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},

(2.5)

which remarkably coincide with dynamic structures of the above-mentioned dynamic factor model with
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}=\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top}
,
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}=\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t}
,
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{U}^{\top}=\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top}
, and
\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\top}\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{0}_{r\times(p-r)}
.

2.2
Common response and predictor factors

For model (
2.1
), consider its response and predictor spaces,
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{U})
and
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{V})
, and suppose that their intersection is of dimension
d
, i.e.
\text{rank}(\textbf{[}\mathbf{U}~\mathbf{V}\textbf{]})=2r-d
, with
0\leq d\leq r
.
As a result, when
d\geq 1
, there exist two orthogonal matrices
\mathbf{O}_{1},\mathbf{O}_{2}\in\mathbb{O}^{r\times r}
such that

\mathbf{U}\mathbf{O}_{1}=[\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times r},~~\mathbf{V}\mathbf{O}_{2}=\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times r}~~\text{and}~~\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{0}_{d\times(r-d)},

(2.6)

where the orthonormal matrices
\mathbf{R},\mathbf{P}\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times(r-d)}
represent the
response-specific
and
predictor-specific subspaces
of dimension
r-d
, respectively, the orthonormal matrix
\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times d}
represents the
common subspace
of dimension
d
, and
\mathbf{C}
is orthogonal to
\mathbf{R}
and
\mathbf{P}
.

Let
\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{O}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{O}_{2}
, and model (
2.1
) can be rewritten as

\mathbf{y}_{t}=\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}\mathbf{D}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}.

(2.7)

Let
\mathbf{c}_{t}=\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathbf{r}_{t}=\mathbf{R}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{r-d}
, and
\mathbf{p}_{t}=\mathbf{P}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{r-d}
. Then model (
2.7
) implies

\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{c}_{t}\\
\mathbf{r}_{t}\end{bmatrix}=\mathbf{D}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{c}_{t-1}\\
\mathbf{p}_{t-1}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{C}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\\
\mathbf{R}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\end{bmatrix}.

(2.8)

We call the model in (
2.7
) or equivalently
in (
2.8
) the
vector autoregression with common response and predictor factors
, and
\mathbf{c}_{t}
,
\mathbf{r}_{t}
, and
\mathbf{p}_{t}
are referred to as the
common, response-specific, and predictor-specific factors
, respectively.

The proposed model in (
2.7
)
can provide a better physical interpretation, especially for financial and economic series, than reduced-rank VAR models in (
2.1
) by distinguishing these three types of factors; see Section
8
for empirical evidence.
Moreover, the reduced-rank model in (
2.1
) has
d_{\text{RR}}(p,r)=r(2p-r)
parameters, whereas the proposed model has
d_{\text{CS}}(p,r,d)=r(2p-r)-d(p-(d+1)/2)
parameters. When
r
and
d
are much smaller than
p
, the model complexity is roughly reduced from
2pr
to
2pr-pd
, and hence the corresponding estimation efficiency can be improved; see simulation experiments in Section
7
.

Using tensor operations, we extend the proposed model to general VAR(
\ell
) processes,

\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{A}_{1}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}+\cdots+\mathbf{A}_{\ell}\mathbf{y}_{t-\ell}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}.

(2.9)

The parameter matrices are first rearranged into a tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p\times\ell}
such that its mode-1 matricization is
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(1)}=\textbf{[}\mathbf{A}_{1}~\mathbf{A}_{2}~\cdots~\mathbf{A}_{\ell}\textbf{]}
, and its mode-2 matricization assumes the form
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(2)}=\textbf{[}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{\top}~\mathbf{A}_{2}^{\top}~\cdots~\mathbf{A}_{\ell}^{\top}\textbf{]}
.
Note that the column spaces of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(1)}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(2)}
are the column and row spaces of all parameter matrices, respectively. Suppose that they are of dimensions
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, respectively; that is,
\text{rank}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(1)})=r_{1}
and
\text{rank}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(2)})=r_{2}
, where
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
may not be equal. We have a Tucker decomposition via higher-order singular value decomposition (HOSVD)
(
De Lathauwer et al., 2000
)
,

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{S}$}\times_{1}\mathbf{U}\times_{2}\mathbf{V},

where
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times r_{1}}
and
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times r_{2}}
consist of the top
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
left singular vectors of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(1)}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(2)}
, respectively, the core tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{S}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\times_{1}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\times_{2}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}\times r_{2}\times\ell}
, and
\times_{i}
is the tensor-matrix mode-
i
multiplication defined in Appendix
D
.

Split the mode-1 matricization of
\mathscr{S}
into
\textbf{[}\mathbf{S}_{1}~\mathbf{S}_{2}~\cdots~\mathbf{S}_{\ell}\textbf{]}
with each
\mathbf{S}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}\times r_{2}}
, and then model (
2.9
) becomes

\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{U}\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{S}_{k}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-k}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{or}\hskip 14.22636pt\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}=\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{S}_{k}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-k}+\mathbf{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},

(2.10)

where
\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}
and
\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-k}
are the response and predictor factors, respectively.
Note that
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{V}
are not unique, but the subspaces
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{U})
and
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{V})
, together with their projectors
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}
and
\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^{\top}
, can be uniquely defined.
As in VAR(1), we can interpret model (
2.10
) as a supervised factor model with
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{U})
and
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{V})
being the response and predictor factor spaces, respectively.

Remark 1
.

Let
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{k}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}_{k+1}
and
\mathbf{f}_{t-k}=\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-k-1}
, and then model (
2.10
) can be rewritten into
\mathbf{y}_{t}=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{k}\mathbf{f}_{t-k}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
, i.e. it admits a generalized dynamic factor modeling form in
Forni et al., 2000
;
Forni et al., 2005
. Note that the proposed model is for a supervised problem, while the generalized dynamic factor modeling is fundamentally for an unsupervised one. In addition, the factors and errors in
Forni et al., 2000
;
Forni et al., 2005
are assumed to be uncorrelated in all leads and lags, but those in our model are not.

Suppose that the response and predictor subspaces share a common subspace of dimension
d
, i.e.,
\text{rank}(\textbf{[}\mathbf{U}~\mathbf{V}\textbf{]})=r_{1}+r_{2}-d
, with
0\leq d\leq\min(r_{1},r_{2})
.
Then there exist two matrices,
\mathbf{O}_{1}\in\mathbb{O}^{r_{1}\times r_{1}}
and
\mathbf{O}_{2}\in\mathbb{O}^{r_{2}\times r_{2}}
, such that

\mathbf{U}\mathbf{O}_{1}=[\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times r_{1}}
,
\mathbf{V}\mathbf{O}_{2}=\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times r_{2}}
, and
\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{0}_{d\times(r-d)}
,
where
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times(r_{1}-d)}
,
\mathbf{P}\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times(r_{2}-d)}
, and
\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times d}
are the response-specific, predictor-specific, and common subspaces of dimensions
r_{1}-d
,
r_{2}-d
, and
d
, respectively.
As a result, the parameter tensor can be formulated into

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{D}$}\times_{1}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}\times_{2}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}
,
where
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{D}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{S}$}\times_{1}\mathbf{O}_{1}^{\top}\times_{2}\mathbf{O}_{2}^{\top}
.
Furthermore, let
\mathbf{c}_{t}=\mathbf{C}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathbf{r}_{t}=\mathbf{R}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}-d}
, and
\mathbf{p}_{t}=\mathbf{P}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{2}-d}
, and then model (
2.10
) has the form

\mathbf{y}_{t}=\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{D}_{k}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-k}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{or}\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{c}_{t}\\
\mathbf{r}_{t}\end{bmatrix}=\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{D}_{k}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{c}_{t-k}\\
\mathbf{p}_{t-k}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{C}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\\
\mathbf{R}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\end{bmatrix},

(2.11)

where each
\mathbf{D}_{k}
is a
r_{1}
-by-
r_{2}
matrix such that
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{D}$}_{(1)}=\textbf{[}\mathbf{D}_{1}~\mathbf{D}_{2}~\cdots~\mathbf{D}_{\ell}\textbf{]}
.
The model (
2.11
) defines a general vector autoregression with common response and predictor factors, and
\mathbf{c}_{t}
,
\mathbf{r}_{t}
, and
\mathbf{p}_{t}
are the common, response-specific, and predictor-specific factors, respectively.

For the parameter tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p\times\ell}
, dimension reduction is conducted along the first two modes, and when the lag order
\ell
is large, it is also of interest to further restrict the parameter space along the third mode. Specifically, assume that
\text{rank}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(3)})=r_{3}
, and we have the Tucker decomposition:
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{D}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\times_{3}\mathbf{L}
and

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\times_{1}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}\times_{2}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}\times_{3}\mathbf{L},

(2.12)

where
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}\times r_{2}\times r_{3}}
is the core tensor and
\mathbf{L}\in\mathbb{O}^{\ell\times r_{3}}
is the lag factor matrix.
Similarly, this additional low-rankness along the third mode would lead to a lag-specific factor.
The number of parameters under the low-rank structure is
d_{\text{CS}}(p,\ell,r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},d)=r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}+r_{1}(p-r_{1})+r_{2}(p-r_{2})+r_{3}(\ell-r_{3})-d(p-(d+1)/2)
, while model (
2.9
) has
p^{2}\ell
parameters.

3
High-Dimensional Estimation Methods

3.1
Regularized estimation and gradient descent algorithm

Consider the observed sequence,
\{\mathbf{y}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{T}\}
, generated by the VAR(1) model in (
2.7
), and suppose that both
r
and
d
are known.
Our aim is to estimate the parameter matrix

\mathbf{A}:=\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{R},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{D})=\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}\mathbf{D}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}^{\top}=\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{D}_{11}&\mathbf{D}_{12}\\
\mathbf{D}_{21}&\mathbf{D}_{22}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{C}^{\top}\\
\mathbf{P}^{\top}\end{bmatrix},

where
\mathbf{D}_{11}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
,
\mathbf{D}_{12}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(r-d)}
,
\mathbf{D}_{21}\in\mathbb{R}^{(r-d)\times d}
, and
\mathbf{D}_{22}\in\mathbb{R}^{(r-d)\times(r-d)}
are four blocks of
\mathbf{D}
.
Let
\mathbf{Y}=\textbf{[}\mathbf{y}_{1}~\cdots~\mathbf{y}_{T}\textbf{]}
and
\mathbf{X}=\textbf{[}\mathbf{y}_{0}~\cdots~\mathbf{y}_{T-1}\textbf{]}
, and the squared loss function is

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{R},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{D})=\frac{1}{2T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}\mathbf{D}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{2T}\left\|\mathbf{Y}-\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}\mathbf{D}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}.

(3.1)

With
a,b>0
being regularization parameters, the components in (
2.7
) can be estimated by

\begin{split}\left(\widehat{\mathbf{C}},\widehat{\mathbf{R}},\widehat{\mathbf{P}},\widehat{\mathbf{D}}\right)=&\argmin_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times d},\mathbf{D}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r},\\
\mathbf{R},\mathbf{P}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times(r-d)}\end{subarray}}\Bigg\{\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{R},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{D})\\
&+\frac{a}{2}\left\|\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}^{\top}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}-b^{2}\mathbf{I}_{r}\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}+\frac{a}{2}\left\|\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}^{\top}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}-b^{2}\mathbf{I}_{r}\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\Bigg\}.\end{split}

(3.2)

The above estimation method is motivated by
Han et al., 2021
for low-rank tensor estimation, and the regularization terms
\|\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}^{\top}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}-b^{2}\mathbf{I}_{r}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}
and
\|\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}^{\top}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}-b^{2}\mathbf{I}_{r}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}
are used to keep
\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}
and
\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}
from being singular and to balance the scaling of these components.
It is noteworthy that
\textbf{[}\widehat{\mathbf{C}}~\widehat{\mathbf{R}}\textbf{]}^{\top}\textbf{[}\widehat{\mathbf{C}}~\widehat{\mathbf{R}}\textbf{]}=\textbf{[}\widehat{\mathbf{C}}~\widehat{\mathbf{P}}\textbf{]}^{\top}\textbf{[}\widehat{\mathbf{C}}~\widehat{\mathbf{P}}\textbf{]}=b^{2}\mathbf{I}_{r}
.
Moreover, the estimated parameter matrix
\mathbf{A}(\widehat{\mathbf{C}},\widehat{\mathbf{R}},\widehat{\mathbf{P}},\widehat{\mathbf{D}})
is not sensitive to the choices of regularization parameters
a
and
b
, and they are set to one in all our numerical analysis.

We use the gradient descent method to solve the optimization problem in (
3.2
). Specifically, the partial derivatives can be calculated as

\begin{split}\nabla_{\mathbf{C}}\mathcal{L}=&\nabla\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A})(\mathbf{C}\mathbf{D}_{11}^{\top}+\mathbf{P}\mathbf{D}_{12}^{\top})+\nabla\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A})^{\top}(\mathbf{C}\mathbf{D}_{11}+\mathbf{R}\mathbf{D}_{21}),\nabla_{\mathbf{D}}\mathcal{L}=\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}^{\top}\nabla\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A})\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]},\\
\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{L}=&\nabla\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A})\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}\textbf{[}\mathbf{D}_{21}~\mathbf{D}_{22}\textbf{]}^{\top},~\text{and}~\nabla_{\mathbf{P}}\mathcal{L}=\nabla\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A})^{\top}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}\textbf{[}\mathbf{D}_{12}^{\top}~\mathbf{D}_{22}^{\top}\textbf{]}^{\top},\end{split}

(3.3)

where
\nabla\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A})=T^{-1}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{Y})\mathbf{X}^{\top}=T^{-1}(\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}\mathbf{D}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}^{\top}\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{Y})\mathbf{X}^{\top}
.
Given an initial estimator
\mathbf{A}^{(0)}=\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}^{(0)}~\mathbf{R}^{(0)}\textbf{]}\mathbf{D}^{(0)}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}^{(0)}~\mathbf{P}^{(0)}\textbf{]}^{\top}
and a step size
\eta
, we can then design a gradient descent algorithm to search for the minimizer of (
3.2
); see Algorithm
1
.

Algorithm 1

Gradient descent algorithm for VAR(1) model with known
r
and
d

1:
Input
:
\mathbf{Y}
,
\mathbf{X}
, step size
\eta
, number of iteration
I
, initial values
\mathbf{C}^{(0)}
,
\mathbf{R}^{(0)}
,
\mathbf{P}^{(0)}
, and
\mathbf{D}^{(0)}

2:
for

i=0,\dots,I-1

3:
\mathbf{C}^{(i+1)}=\mathbf{C}^{(i)}-\eta\nabla_{\mathbf{C}}\mathcal{L}^{(i)}-\eta a\big[2\mathbf{C}^{(i)}(\mathbf{C}^{(i)\top}\mathbf{C}^{(i)}-b^{2}\mathbf{I}_{d})+\mathbf{R}^{(i)}\mathbf{R}^{(i)\top}\mathbf{C}^{(i)}+\mathbf{P}^{(i)}\mathbf{P}^{(i)\top}\mathbf{C}^{(i)}\big]

4:
\mathbf{R}^{(i+1)}=\mathbf{R}^{(i)}-\eta\nabla_{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{L}^{(i)}-\eta a\big[\mathbf{R}^{(i)}(\mathbf{R}^{(i)\top}\mathbf{R}^{(i)}-b^{2}\mathbf{I}_{r-d})+\mathbf{C}^{(i)}\mathbf{C}^{(i)\top}\mathbf{R}^{(i)}\big]

5:
\mathbf{P}^{(i+1)}=\mathbf{P}^{(i)}-\eta\nabla_{\mathbf{P}}\mathcal{L}^{(i)}-\eta a\big[\mathbf{P}^{(i)}(\mathbf{P}^{(i)\top}\mathbf{P}^{(i)}-b^{2}\mathbf{I}_{r-d})+\mathbf{C}^{(i)}\mathbf{C}^{(i)\top}\mathbf{P}^{(i)}\big]

6:

\mathbf{D}^{(i+1)}=\mathbf{D}^{(i)}-\eta\nabla_{\mathbf{D}}\mathcal{L}^{(i)}

7:
end for

8:
Return
:
\mathbf{A}^{(I)}=\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}^{(I)}~\mathbf{R}^{(I)}\textbf{]}\mathbf{D}^{(I)}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}^{(I)}~\mathbf{P}^{(I)}\textbf{]}^{\top}

3.2
Initialization of the algorithm

The problem in (
3.2
) is non-convex, and the initial values
(\mathbf{C}^{(0)},\mathbf{R}^{(0)},\mathbf{P}^{(0)},\mathbf{D}^{(0)})
play important roles in the algorithm. Hence, we provide a simple spectral initialization method.

Consider model (
2.1
) with
\text{rank}(\textbf{[}\mathbf{U}~\mathbf{V}\textbf{]})=2r-d
and its equivalent form in (
2.7
) with
\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}
and
\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}
being orthonormal matrices. It then holds that
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}=\mathbf{C}\mathbf{C}^{\top}+\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^{\top}
,
\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^{\top}=\mathbf{C}\mathbf{C}^{\top}+\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top}
, and

\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}-\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^{\top}=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^{\top}-\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top}
. Moreover, since
\mathbf{C}
is orthogonal to
\mathbf{R}
and
\mathbf{P}
, we have

\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^{\top})=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})

and

\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})=\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^{\top})
.
It implies that
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{R})
and
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{P})
are the subspaces spanned by the first
r-d
left singular vectors of
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^{\top})
and
\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top})
. In addition,
\mathbf{D}=\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}
and

(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^{\top})(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}+\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^{\top})(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^{\top})(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top})=2\mathbf{C}\mathbf{C}^{\top}
.

The above finding motivates us to use a reduced-rank VAR estimation
(
Velu and Reinsel, 2013
)
to construct an initialization. Specifically, denote by
\widehat{\mathbf{H}}\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times r}
the first
r
eigenvectors of
\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{X}^{\top}(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\top})^{-1}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Y}^{\top}
, corresponding to the
r
largest eigenvalues in the decreasing order, and then the reduced-rank VAR estimation has an explicit form of

\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{RR}}(r)=\argmin_{\text{rank}(\mathbf{A})\leq r}\|\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\|_{\text{F}}^{2}=\widehat{\mathbf{H}}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{\top}\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{X}^{\top}(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}^{\top})^{-1}.

(3.4)

As a result, the following procedure is suggested for initialization:

(i.)

Conduct SVD to the reduced-rank VAR estimator:
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{RR}}(r)=\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}^{\top}
;

(ii.)

Calculate the top
r-d
left singular vectors of
\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}^{\top})
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}^{\top}(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}^{\top})
, and denote them by
\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}
, respectively;

(iii.)

Calculate the top
d
eigenvectors of
(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}^{\top})(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}^{\top})(\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}+\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}^{\top})(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}^{\top})(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}^{\top})
, and denote it by
\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}
;

(iv.)

Calculate
\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}=\textbf{[}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}~\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}\textbf{]}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{RR}}(r)\textbf{[}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}~\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}\textbf{]}
;

(v.)

Set the initialization to
\mathbf{C}^{(0)}=b\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}
,
\mathbf{R}^{(0)}=b\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}
,
\mathbf{P}^{(0)}=b\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}
, and
\mathbf{D}^{(0)}=b^{-2}\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}
.

3.3
Rank selection and common dimension selection

The rank
r
and common dimension
d
are assumed to be known in the previous two subsections, but they are
unknown in most real applications. Here we propose a two-stage selection procedure to select them and relegate its theoretical justification to Section
4
.

A ridge-type ratio method
(
Xia et al., 2015
)
is first introduced to select the rank
r
, regardless of the existence of the common subspace. Specifically, we first give a pre-specified upper bound
\bar{r}=c\cdot r\ll p
for some
c>1
, and then calculate the estimate
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{\text{RR}}(\bar{r})
in (
3.4
).
Denote by
\widetilde{\sigma}_{1}\geq\widetilde{\sigma}_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\widetilde{\sigma}_{\bar{r}}
its singular values, and then the rank
r
can be selected by

\widehat{r}=\argmin_{1\leq i\leq\bar{r}-1}\frac{\widetilde{\sigma}_{i+1}+s(p,T)}{\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}+s(p,T)},

(3.5)

where the ridge parameter
s(p,T)
is a positive sequence depending on
p
and
T
.

The proposed method is not sensitive to the choice of
\bar{r}
as long as it is greater than
r
. Thus, for large datasets with a large dimension
p
, we can choose the upper bound
\bar{r}
to be reasonably large but much smaller than
p
. When
p
is small, we may even simply set
\bar{r}
to
p
. On the other hand, the ridge parameter
s(p,T)
is essential for consistent rank selection. We suggest using
s(p,T)=\sqrt{p\log(T)/(10T)}
, according to Theorem
3
in Section
4.3
, and its satisfactory performance is observed in our simulation experiments of Section
7
.

Remark 2
.

Note that we do not consider the case of
r=0
throughout this article as it implies that the time series data is a pure white noise sequence, and the ratio estimator naturally rules it out. To formally test whether
r=0
, one may apply the high-dimensional white noise test
(
Li et al., 2019
;
Tsay, 2020
)
.

Next, we consider the selection of the common
dimension
d
in model (
2.7
).
Denote by
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}(r,d)
the estimator obtained from Algorithm
1
with the rank
r
and common dimension
d
, and then the Bayesian information criterion (BIC) can be constructed below,

\text{BIC}(r,d)=Tp\log(\|\mathbf{Y}-\widehat{\mathbf{A}}(r,d)\mathbf{X}\|_{\text{F}}^{2})+d_{\text{CS}}(p,r,d)\log(T),

(3.6)

where
d_{\text{CS}}(p,r,d)=r(2p-r)-d(p-(d+1)/2)
is the number of free parameters.
As a result, given
r
, the common dimension
d
can be selected by

\widehat{d}=\argmin_{0\leq k\leq r}\text{BIC}(r,d)
.
Note that the BIC in (
3.6
) can also be used to select
r
and
d
simultaneously, but it would be time-consuming in practice.

3.4
The case of VAR(
\ell
) models

This subsection extends the proposed methodology to VAR(
\ell
) models with common response and predictor factors.
Suppose that
(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3})
and
d
are known. To estimate the parameter tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\times_{1}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}\times_{2}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}\times_{3}\mathbf{L}
, the loss function is

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{R},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{L},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$})=(2T)^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\times_{1}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}\times_{2}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}\times_{3}\mathbf{L})_{(1)}\mathbf{x}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}
,
where
\mathbf{x}_{t}=\textbf{[}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}^{\top}~\dots~\mathbf{y}_{t-\ell}^{\top}\textbf{]}^{\top}
. With regularization parameters
a,b>0
, we can use a gradient descent algorithm to find the following estimators

\displaystyle\left(\widehat{\mathbf{C}},\widehat{\mathbf{R}},\widehat{\mathbf{P}},\widehat{\mathbf{L}},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{G}}$}\right)=

\displaystyle\argmin_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times d},\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times(r_{1}-d)},\\
\mathbf{P}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times(r_{2}-d)},\mathbf{L}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times r_{3}},\\
\scalebox{0.75}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}\times r_{2}\times r_{3}}\end{subarray}}\Bigg\{\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{R},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{L},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$})+\frac{a}{2}\left\|\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}^{\top}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}-b^{2}\mathbf{I}_{r_{1}}\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt+\frac{a}{2}\left\|\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}^{\top}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}-b^{2}\mathbf{I}_{r_{2}}\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}+\frac{a}{2}\left\|\mathbf{L}^{\top}\mathbf{L}-b^{2}\mathbf{I}_{r_{3}}\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\Bigg\}.

For initialization, consider the rank-constrained estimator
(
Wang et al., 2022
)

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widetilde{A}}$}_{\text{RR}}=\argmin_{\text{rank}(\scalebox{0.7}{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}}_{(i)})=r_{i}}(2T)^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(1)}\mathbf{x}_{t}\|_{2}^{2},

and apply the similar initialization method in Section
3.2
to obtain
(\mathbf{C}^{(0)},\mathbf{R}^{(0)},\mathbf{P}^{(0)},\mathbf{L}^{(0)},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}^{(0)})
. In addition, the ridge-type rank selection and the common dimension selection via BIC can also be extended to VAR(
\ell
) models. For brevity, the algorithm and implementation details are relegated to Appendix
D.2
.

4
Computational and Statistical Convergence Analysis

Sections
4.1
and
4.2
establish the computational and statistical convergence for the VAR(1) model, respectively. Section
4.3
studies the consistency of the rank and common dimension selection, and Section
4.4

provides the theoretical justification for the VAR(
\ell
) model. In what follows, we denote
\mathbf{A}^{*}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}
as the ground truth of the parameter matrix and tensor.

4.1
Computational convergence analysis

The optimization problem in (
3.2
) is non-convex, and it is challenging to establish the convergence analysis of Algorithm
1
.
To solve it, we introduce some regulatory conditions.

Definition 1
.

A function
\mathcal{L}(\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is restricted strongly convex with parameter
\alpha
and restricted strongly smooth with parameter
\beta
, if for any matrices
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
of rank
r
,

\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\|_{\textup{F}}^{2}\leq\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A})-\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime})-\left\langle\nabla\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime}),\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\right\rangle\leq\frac{\beta}{2}\|\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\|_{\textup{F}}^{2}.

(4.1)

Definition 2
.

For the given rank
r
, common dimension
d
, and the true parameter matrix
\mathbf{A}^{*}
, the deviation bound is defined as

\xi(r,d)=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\textup{\bf{[}}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textup{\bf{]}},\textup{\bf{[}}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textup{\bf{]}}\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times r},\mathbf{D}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r},\|\mathbf{D}\|_{\textup{F}}=1\end{subarray}}\left\langle\nabla\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{A}^{*}),\textup{\bf{[}}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textup{\bf{]}}\mathbf{D}\textup{\bf{[}}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textup{\bf{]}}^{\top}\right\rangle.

(4.2)

The restricted strong convexity and smoothness of Definition
1
are essential in establishing the convergence analysis for many non-convex optimization problems; see
Jain and Kar, 2017
and references therein.
The deviation bound
\xi(r,d)
in Definition
2
characterizes the magnitude of statistical noises projected onto a low-dimensional space of matrices with rank
r
and common dimension
d
, and we can treat it as a statistical error as in
Han et al., 2021
.

For the true parameter matrix
\mathbf{A}^{*}
, denote its largest and smallest singular values and its condition number by
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{*})
,
\sigma_{r}=\sigma_{r}(\mathbf{A}^{*})
, and
\kappa=\sigma_{1}/\sigma_{r}
, respectively.
Assuming that both
r
and
d
are known, we state the convergence analysis of Algorithm
1
below.

Theorem 1
.

Suppose that the loss function
\mathcal{L}(\cdot)
satisfies the restricted strong convexity and smoothness of Definition
1
and the deviation bound in Definition
2
.
If
\|\mathbf{A}^{(0)}-\mathbf{A}^{*}\|_{\textup{F}}\lesssim\sigma_{r}
,
a\asymp\alpha\sigma_{1}^{2/3}\kappa^{-2}
,
b\asymp\sigma_{1}^{1/3}
, and
\eta=\eta_{0}\alpha^{-1}\kappa^{2}\sigma_{1}^{-4/3}
with
\eta_{0}
being a positive constant not greater than
1/260
, then it holds that, for all
i\geq 1
,

\|\mathbf{A}^{(i)}-\mathbf{A}^{*}\|_{\textup{F}}^{2}\lesssim\kappa^{2}(1-C\eta_{0}\alpha\beta^{-1}\kappa^{-2})^{i}\|\mathbf{A}^{(0)}-\mathbf{A}^{*}\|_{\textup{F}}^{2}+\kappa^{2}\alpha^{-2}\xi^{2}(r,d).

(4.3)

For the upper bound in Theorem
1
, the first term corresponds to optimization errors, while the second term is related to statistical errors.
From Theorem
1
, the estimation error decreases toward a statistical limit exponentially with respect to iterations.
Moreover, when the parameters
\alpha
,
\sigma_{1}
, and
\kappa
are bounded away from zero and infinity, the tuning parameters
a
,
b
, and
\eta
would be at a constant level and, hence, do not depend on
p
and
T
.

4.2
Statistical convergence analysis

Consider VAR(1) models in (
2.1
) and (
2.7
). We first state some general conditions.

Assumption 1
.

The parameter matrix
\mathbf{A}^{*}
has a spectral radius strictly less than one.

Assumption 2
.

The error term is
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}=\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t}
, where
\{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t}\}
are
i.i.d.
random vectors with
\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t})=\mathbf{0}
and
\textup{var}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t})=\mathbf{I}_{p}
. Moreover, the entries
(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{it})_{1\leq i\leq p}
of
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t}
are mutually independent and
\tau^{2}
-sub-Gaussian, i.e.
\mathbb{E}[\exp(\mu\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{it})]\leq\exp(\tau^{2}\mu^{2}/2)
for any
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
and
1\leq i\leq p
.

Remark 3
.

Assumption
1
is sufficient and necessary for the existence of a unique strictly stationary solution to model (
2.1
) with any finite
p
, and this is consistent with the non-asymptotic framework used in this article. For the case with
p\rightarrow\infty
, we may refer to
Zhu et al., 2017
for the definition of strict stationarity, which is given via a mechanism similar to the Cramer-Wold device. Moreover, the Gaussian condition is commonly used in the literature of high-dimensional time series
(
Basu and Michailidis, 2015
)
, while the sub-Gaussian condition in Assumption
2
is more general.

In the decomposition in (
2.7
), intuitively, the response-specific and predictor-specific subspaces
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times(r-d)}
and
\mathbf{P}\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times(r-d)}
cannot be too close so that we can separate the common subspace
\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times d}
out successfully.
Here we use the
\sin\theta
distance for two spaces.
Specifically, let
s_{1}\geq\dots\geq s_{r-d}\geq 0
be the singular values of
\mathbf{R}^{\top}\mathbf{P}
. Then, the canonical angles between
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{R})
and
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{P})
can be defined as

\theta_{i}(\mathbf{R},\mathbf{P})=\arccos(s_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq r-d
.
The following condition is added to the smallest canonical angle between
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{R})
and
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{P})
.

Assumption 3
.

There exists a constant
g_{\min}>0
such that
\sin\theta_{1}(\mathbf{R}^{*},\mathbf{P}^{*})\geq g_{\min}
.

Furthermore, we quantify the temporal and cross-sectional dependency as in
Basu and Michailidis, 2015
.
For any
z\in\mathbb{C}
, let
\mathcal{A}(z)=\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{A}^{*}z
be the matrix polynomial, where
\mathbb{C}
is the set of all complex numbers. Let
\mu_{\min}(\mathcal{A})=\min_{|z|=1}\lambda_{\min}(\mathcal{A}^{\dagger}(z)\mathcal{A}(z))
and
\mu_{\max}(\mathcal{A})=\max_{|z|=1}\lambda_{\max}(\mathcal{A}^{\dagger}(z)\mathcal{A}(z))
, where
\mathcal{A}^{\dagger}(z)
is the conjugate transpose of
\mathcal{A}(z)
. Moreover, denote

\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}=\frac{\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})}{2\mu_{\max}(\mathcal{A})},~\beta_{\textup{RSS}}=\frac{3\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})}{2\mu_{\min}(\mathcal{A})},~M_{1}=\frac{\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})}{\mu_{\min}^{1/2}(\mathcal{A})},~\text{and}~M_{2}=\frac{\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})\mu_{\max}(\mathcal{A})}{\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})\mu_{\min}(\mathcal{A})}.

(4.4)

Based on them, we have the following statistical convergence analysis for Algorithm
1
.

Theorem 2
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
–
3
hold,

T\gtrsim\max(\tau^{4},\tau^{2})M_{2}^{-2}p
, and the conditions in Theorem
1
are satisfied with
\alpha=\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}
and
\beta=\beta_{\textup{RSS}}
. Then, after
I
-th iteration with
I\gtrsim\log(\kappa^{-1}\sigma_{1}^{-1/3}g_{\min})/\log(1-C\eta_{0}\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}\beta_{\textup{RSS}}^{-1}\kappa^{-2})
, with probability at least
1-4\exp[-CM_{2}^{2}\min(\tau^{-2},\tau^{-4})T]-2\exp(-Cp)
,

\|\mathbf{A}^{(I)}-\mathbf{A}^{*}\|_{\textup{F}}\lesssim\kappa\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}^{-1}\tau^{2}M_{1}\sqrt{d_{\textup{CS}}(p,r,d)/T}.

(4.5)

The above theorem gives an estimation error bound after a sufficiently large number of iterations.
When the quantities of
\kappa,\sigma_{1},g_{\min},\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}
and
\beta_{\textup{RSS}}
are bounded away from zero and infinity, the required number of iterations does not depend on the dimension
p
or the sample size
T
, and this makes sure that the proposed algorithm can be applied to large datasets without any difficulty.
Moreover, the estimated parameter matrix from Algorithm
1
has the convergence rate of
\sqrt{d_{\text{CS}}(p,r,d)/T}
, while the reduced-rank VAR estimation has the rate of
\sqrt{d_{\text{RR}}(p,r)/T}

(
Negahban and Wainwright, 2011
)
. Note that
d_{\text{RR}}(p,r)-d_{\text{CS}}(p,r,d)
roughly equals to
pd
when both
r
and
d
are much smaller than
p
. This efficiency improvement is due to the fact that the proposed methodology takes into account the possible common subspace.

4.3
Rank and common dimension selection consistency

In this section, we provide theoretical justifications for rank and common dimension selection. First, we establish the rank selection consistency for the ridge-type ratio in (
3.5
).

Theorem 3
.

Under Assumptions
1
and
2
, if
T\gtrsim\max(\tau^{4},\tau^{2})M_{2}^{-2}p
,
\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}^{-1}\tau^{2}M_{1}\sqrt{p\bar{r}/T}=o(s(p,T))
,

s(p,T)=o(\sigma_{r}^{-1}\min_{1\leq i\leq r-1}\sigma_{j+1}/\sigma_{j})
,
and
r<\bar{r}
, then
\mathbb{P}(\widehat{r}=r)\to 1
as
T\to\infty
.

The conditions in this theorem reduce to
s^{-1}(p,T)\sqrt{p/T}\to 0
and
s(p,T)\to 0
as
T\to\infty
, when
\sigma_{1}
,
\sigma_{r}^{-1}
,
\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}^{-1}
,
\tau
, and
M_{1}
are bounded.
Moreover, the required sample size in Theorem
3
is the same as that for the estimation consistency in Theorem
2
.

Given that the rank
r
is known, the following theorem provides theoretical justifications for the proposed BIC in (
3.6
).

Theorem 4
.

Suppose the conditions in Theorem
2
hold. Then,
\mathbb{P}(\widehat{d}=d)\to 1
as
T\to\infty
.

4.4
Convergence analysis for VAR(
\ell
) models

This subsection extends the convergence analysis of the gradient descent algorithm for VAR(1) to VAR(
\ell
). We refer the readers to Appendix
D
for the detailed algorithm and implementation.
The computational convergence analysis can be extended from that in Section
4.1
, and is omitted to save space. Here, we focus on the statistical convergence.

For the VAR (
\ell
) model in (
2.9
), define the matrix polynomial
\mathcal{A}(z)=\mathbf{I}_{p}-\mathbf{A}_{1}^{*}z-\mathbf{A}_{2}^{*}z^{2}-\cdots-\mathbf{A}_{\ell}^{*}z^{\ell}
, where
z\in\mathbb{C}
, and its stationarity condition is given below.

Assumption 4
.

The determinant of
\mathcal{A}(z)
is not equal to zero for all
|z|<1
.

Denote by
\bar{\sigma}=\max_{1\leq i\leq 3}\sigma_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(i)})
,
\underline{\sigma}=\min_{1\leq i\leq 3}\sigma_{r_{i}}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}_{(i)})
, and
\kappa=\bar{\sigma}/\underline{\sigma}
the largest and smallest singular values and the condition number of the true parameter tensor, respectively.
As in Section
4.2
, we can similarly define the quantities,
\mu_{\min}(\mathcal{A})
,
\mu_{\max}(\mathcal{A})
,
\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}
,
\beta_{\textup{RSS}}
,
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
.
The statistical convergence analysis is given below.

Theorem 5
.

Suppose that Assumptions
2
–
4
hold,
T\gtrsim\max(\tau^{4},\tau^{2})M_{2}^{-2}(pr_{1}+pr_{2}+\ell r_{3})
,
a\asymp\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}\bar{\sigma}^{3/4}\kappa^{-2}
,
b\asymp\bar{\sigma}^{1/4}
, and
\eta=\eta_{0}\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}^{-1}\kappa^{2}\bar{\sigma}^{-3/2}
. Then, after
I
-th iteration of the gradient descent with

I\gtrsim\log(\kappa^{-1}\bar{\sigma}^{-3/4}g_{\min})/\log(1-C\eta_{0}\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}\beta_{\textup{RSS}}^{-1}\kappa^{-2})
, with probability at least
1-4\exp[-CM_{2}^{2}\min(\tau^{-2},\tau^{-4})T]-2\exp[-C(r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}+pr_{1}+pr_{2}+\ell r_{3})]
,

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{(I)}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{\textup{F}}\lesssim\kappa\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}^{-1}\tau^{2}M_{1}\sqrt{d_{\textup{CS}}(p,\ell,r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},d)/T}.

(4.6)

From the theorem, the estimation efficiency can be achieved by considering the common structure between
\mathcal{M}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(1)})
and
\mathcal{M}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(2)})
.
We can also establish the consistency for rank and common dimension selection, but it is omitted for brevity.

5
Diverging Eigenvalue Effect

5.1
Diverging eigenvalue and elimination transformation

In many high-dimensional time series data, it is common to observe the diverging eigenvalue effect in
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{y}}=\text{var}(\mathbf{y}_{t})
: all diagonal entries in
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{y}}
are bounded, but the leading eigenvalues of
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{y}}
are diverging to infinity with
p
increasing. This phenomenon implies the pervasive cross-sectional dependency and has been well studied in the econometrics literature of factor modeling with common factors and idiosyncratic errors
(
Bai and Ng, 2008
)
.

If we model the data with the diverging eigenvalue effect via a VAR(1) model in (
1.1
), the relationship between
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{y}}
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
, namely

\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{y}}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{A}^{\top}+\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
,
implies that the diverging eigenvalues of
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{y}}
may be splitted into the autoregression part and white noise part. In other words, at least one of the conditional expectation of the response and white noise have strong cross-sectional dependence.
Under Assumption
1
for the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{A}
, some singular values of
\mathbf{A}
are allowed to diverge. However, if
\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})
is diverging, the estimation error bound in Theorem
2
is
O_{p}(\mu_{\max}(\mathcal{A})\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})\sqrt{pr/T})
, resulting in a much larger sample size requirement. To this end, we propose an elimination transformation to remove the diverging eigenvalue effect in white noise errors.

Remark 4
.

For
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{V}^{\top}
, the diverging singular values exist typically when
\mathbf{U}
is pervasive such that the response factor is related to most or even all of the variables, but the predictor loading
\mathbf{V}
is highly sparse. For example, consider the rank-1
\mathbf{A}=0.9\mathbf{1}_{p}(1,0,0,\cdots,0)^{\top}
, where
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A})=0.9\sqrt{p}
and the nonzero eigenvalue of
\mathbf{A}
is 0.9. In this case, the diverging eigenvalues in
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{y}}
may come from the pervasive dependence on the conditional expectation of the response;
see the empirical evidence in Section
8
.

Based on the decomposition in (
2.2
),
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1t}:=\mathbf{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
is involved in the low-dimensional autoregressive model, and
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2t}:=\mathbf{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
, where
\mathbf{U}_{\perp}\in\mathbb{O}^{p\times(p-r)}
such that
\mathbf{U}^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{\perp}=\mathbf{0}
, is not related to the parameter matrix
\mathbf{A}
. Hence, when estimating
\mathbf{A}
, it is beneficial to eliminate the diverging eigenvalue effect in
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2t}
and preserve information in
\mathcal{M}({\mathbf{U}})
to avoid model bias.
Suppose
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}:=\text{var}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2t})
has
K
diverging eigenvalues, i.e.,

\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}=\mathbf{K}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}^{K}\mathbf{K}^{\top}+\mathbf{K}_{\perp}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}^{-K}\mathbf{K}^{\top}_{\perp}
,
where
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}^{K}=\text{diag}(\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}),\dots,\lambda_{K}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}))
and
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}^{-K}=\text{diag}(\lambda_{K+1}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}),\dots,\lambda_{p-r}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}))
contains the diverging and bounded eigenvalues, respectively, and
\mathbf{K}
and
\mathbf{K}_{\perp}
are the corresponding eigenvectors. The transformation

\mathbf{T}_{K}=\mathbf{K}(\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}^{K})^{-1/2}\mathbf{K}^{\top}+\mathbf{K}_{\perp}\mathbf{K}_{\perp}^{\top}
can remove the diverging eigenvalue effect in
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}
, since

\text{var}(\mathbf{T}_{K}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2t})=\mathbf{T}_{K}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}\mathbf{T}_{K}=\mathbf{K}\mathbf{K}^{\top}+\mathbf{K}_{\perp}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}^{-K}\mathbf{K}_{\perp}^{\top}=[\mathbf{K}~\mathbf{K}_{\perp}]\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{0}_{K\times(p-K)}\\
\mathbf{0}_{(p-K)\times K}&\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}^{-K}\end{bmatrix}[\mathbf{K}~\mathbf{K}_{\perp}]^{\top}.

(5.1)

In order to preserve the informative factor loading in
\mathbf{U}
when estimating
\mathbf{A}
, we consider the
\mathbf{U}
-preserved transformation

\mathbf{T}_{U}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{\top}+\mathbf{U}_{\perp}\mathbf{T}_{K}\mathbf{U}_{\perp}^{\top}

and denote
\widebar{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{t}=\mathbf{T}_{U}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
such that

\text{var}(\widebar{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{t})=\text{var}(\mathbf{U}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1t}+\mathbf{U}_{\perp}\mathbf{T}_{K}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2t})=[\mathbf{U}~\mathbf{U}_{\perp}]\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}&\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1t}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2t}^{\top}\mathbf{T}_{K}]\\
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{T}_{K}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2t}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1t}^{\top}]&\mathbf{T}_{K}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}\mathbf{T}_{K}\end{bmatrix}[\mathbf{U}~\mathbf{U}_{\perp}]^{\top}.

(5.2)

If the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1}}
are bounded, all eigenvalues of
\text{var}(\widebar{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{t})
are bounded. In other words, the diverging eigenvalue effects of
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
in
\mathcal{M}^{\perp}(\mathbf{U})
can be removed.
By the
\mathbf{U}
-preseved property of the transformation
\mathbf{T}_{U}
, we have that
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{T}_{U}\mathbf{A}
. Thus, the reduced-rank VAR model in (
2.1
) implies that

\widebar{\mathbf{y}}_{t}\equiv\mathbf{T}_{U}\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{T}_{U}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}+\mathbf{T}_{U}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}+\widebar{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{t}
,
in which the diverging eigenvalue effects in the white noise innovations are alleviated.

5.2
Estimation methodology

First, the reduced-rank VAR(1) model can be formulated to the factor model in (
2.3
)

\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}=\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{S}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{1t})+\mathbf{U}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2t}:=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{f}_{t}+\mathbf{U}_{\perp}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2t},

(5.3)

where
\mathbf{f}_{t}
is an
r
-dimensional dynamic factor and
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2t}
is a white noise.
Following the literature of factor models with dynamically dependent factors and white noise errors
(
Lam and Yao, 2012
;
Gao and Tsay, 2022
)
, we consider the autocovariance matrices
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{y}}(j)=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{y}_{t}\mathbf{y}_{t-j}^{\top}]
, for
j\geq 0
. It follows from (
5.3
) that

\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{y}}(j)=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{f}}(j)\mathbf{U}^{\top}+\mathbf{U}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{f}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}(j)\mathbf{U}_{\perp}^{\top}
, for
j\geq 1
,
where
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{f}}(j)=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{f}_{t}\mathbf{f}_{t-j}^{\top}]
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{f}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}(j)=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{f}_{t}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2,t-j}^{\top}]
. For a prespecified integer
N>0
, define
\mathbf{M}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{y}}(j)\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{y}}(j)^{\top}
,
and
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{U})
is the subspace spanned by the first
r
eigenvectors of
\mathbf{M}
. Given
\mathbf{U}_{\perp}
, the covariance matrix of
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2t}
is
\mathbf{U}_{\perp}^{\top}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{U}_{\perp}
and its first
K
eigenvectors are
\mathbf{K}
.

Therefore, we can first obtain the estimates
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{\perp}
by calculating the first
r
and last
p-r
eigenvectors of
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{y}}(j)\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{y}}(j)^{\top}
, respectively, where each sample autocovariance is calculated via
\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{y}}(j)=(T-j)^{-1}\sum_{t=j+1}^{T}\mathbf{y}_{t}\mathbf{y}_{t-j}^{\top}
. The diverging eigenvalues and the corresponding eigenvectors of
\text{var}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2t})
can be estimated by the first
K
eigenvalues and eigenvectors of
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{\perp}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{y}}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{\perp}
, denoted by
\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{K}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{K}}
.
Then, we can obtain the estimated transformations
\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{K}=\widehat{\mathbf{K}}(\widehat{\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{K}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{2}})^{-1/2}\widehat{\mathbf{K}}^{\top}+\widehat{\mathbf{K}}_{\perp}\widehat{\mathbf{K}}_{\perp}^{\top}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{U}=\widehat{\mathbf{U}}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}+\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{\perp}\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{K}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}^{\top}_{\perp}
, and apply the transformation to obtain
\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t}=\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{U}\mathbf{y}_{t}
. Let the matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}=[\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{1}~\cdots~\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{T}]\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times T}
collect all transformed response vectors, and we can use the transformed response
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}
and the original predictor
\mathbf{X}
in the methods described in Section
3
to complete the estimation procedure.

When the number of factors
r
in (
5.3
) is unknown, we may use the eigenvalue ridge-type ratios
(
Xia et al., 2015
)
of
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}
to estimate it numerically. To determine the number of diverging eigenvalues
K
, we may also calculate the eigenvalue ridge-type ratios of
\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{\perp}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{\mathbf{y}}\widehat{\mathbf{U}}_{\perp}
, or select the diverging eigenvalues based on a threshold
p^{\delta}
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
.

Finally, for the general VAR(
\ell
) model, we can also use the same factor model estimation method to obtain
\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{U}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t}
, and apply the transformed response
\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t}
and the original predictors
\mathbf{y}_{t-1},\cdots,\mathbf{y}_{t-\ell}
to the proposed estimation procedure in Section
3.4
.

5.3
Theoretical results

In this subsection, we focus on the VAR(1) model, as the results can easily be extended to the general VAR(
\ell
) models.
When the singular values of
\mathbf{A}^{*}
are diverging, the spectral measurements
\mu_{\max}(\mathcal{A})
and
\mu^{-1}_{\min}(\mathcal{A})
may be diverging as well.
In the setting with diverging eigenvalue effect, representing the cross-sectional and temporal dependency in
\mathbf{y}_{t}
by the spectral measurements in Section
4
may result in a loose result. As we assume the leading eigenvalues of
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{y}}
are diverging,
it is more natural to impose the following assumptions on the explicit diverging rates of the specific components in the model.

Assumption 5
.

All nonzero singular values of
\mathbf{A}^{*}
scale as
p^{\delta_{s}}
for some
\delta_{s}\in[0,1/2]
.

Assumption 6
.

The first
r
eigenvalues of
\mathbf{U}^{*\top}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\mathbf{U}^{*}
scale as
p^{\delta_{u}}
for some
\delta_{u}\in[0,1]
. The first
K
eigenvalues of
\mathbf{U}_{\perp}^{*\top}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\mathbf{U}_{\perp}^{*}
scale as
p^{\delta_{u}^{\prime}}
for some
\delta_{u}^{\prime}\in(0,1]
, and the other eigenvalues are bounded. In addition, the first
r
eigenvalues of
\mathbf{V}^{*\top}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{V}^{*}
scale as
p^{\delta_{v}}
for some
\delta_{v}\in[0,1]
.

The term
\delta_{s}
in Assumption
5
characterizes the strength of diverging singular values in
\mathbf{A}^{*}
. Note that it is possible that
\rho(\mathbf{A}^{*})<1
but
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{*})
diverges to infinity. However, when
d=r
, we must have
\delta_{s}=0
to ensure stationarity. The terms
\delta_{u}
and
\delta_{u}^{\prime}
in Assumption
6
represent the diverging eigenvalue effects of the white noise errors in the response subspace
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{U}^{*})
and its orthogonal complement, respectively. The term
\delta_{v}
characterizes the signal strength in the predictor factor
\mathbf{V}^{*\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}
. Based on these diverging rates, denote

\alpha_{\textup{RSC}}^{\prime}=\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{V}^{*\top}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{V}^{*})/2
,
\beta_{\textup{RSS}}^{\prime}=2\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{V}^{*\top}\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{V}^{*})
and
M_{1}^{\prime}=Cp^{(\delta_{u}+\delta_{v})/2}

as the variants of
\alpha_{\text{RSC}}
,
\beta_{\text{RSS}}
and
M_{1}
defined in Section
4
. These quantities are related to the explicit diverging rates and are more suitable to derive the theory here.

Suppose that the number of diverging eigenvalues
K
are known, we present the theoretical guarantees for the estimation procedures with
\widetilde{\mathbf{Y}}
and
\mathbf{X}
used in Algorithm
1
.

Theorem 6
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
-
3
,
5
and
6
hold,
T\gtrsim\max(\tau^{4},\tau^{2})p
,
\max(\delta_{u},\delta_{v}+2\delta_{s})\geq\delta_{u}^{\prime}
, and the conditions in Theorem
1
are satisfied with
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}_{\textup{RSC}}
and
\beta=\beta^{\prime}_{\textup{RSS}}
. Then, after the
I
-th iteration with
I\gtrsim\log(p^{\delta_{s}/3}g_{\min})/\log(1-C\eta_{0})
, with probability at least
1-4\exp[-C\min(\tau^{-2},\tau^{-4})T]-C\exp(-Cp)
,

\|\mathbf{A}^{(I)}-\mathbf{A}^{*}\|_{\textup{F}}\lesssim(\alpha^{\prime}_{\textup{RSC}})^{-1}\tau^{2}M_{1}^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\textup{CS}}(p,r,d)}{T}}=\tau^{2}p^{(\delta_{u}-\delta_{v})/2}\sqrt{\frac{d_{\textup{CS}}(p,r,d)}{T}}.

(5.4)

Theorem
6
presents the statistical convergence rate of the proposed estimator for the data with the diverging eigenvalue effect. First, the additional signal strengh condition,
\max(\delta_{u},\delta_{v}+2\delta_{s})\geq\delta_{u}^{\prime}
, implies that the signal strength in the low-dimensional dynamic part
\mathbf{U}^{*\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{S}^{*}\mathbf{V}^{*\top}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}+\mathbf{U}^{*\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
is not weaker than that in the white noise part
\mathbf{U}_{\perp}^{*\top}\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{U}_{\perp}^{*\top}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
, such that the proposed factor modeling method can work. Second, if
\tau
is fixed, the upper bound scales as
O_{p}(\sqrt{p^{1+\delta_{u}-\delta_{v}}/T})
. If
\delta_{u}\leq\delta_{v}
, i.e., the strength of the predictor factors is stronger than the that of white noise errors in
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{U}^{*})
, the rate is even faster than that in Theorem
2
. Third, if we ignore the diverging effect in the data and apply the standard estimation procedure in Section
3
, the resulting rate in Theorem
2
, scaling as
O_{p}(\mu_{\max}(\mathcal{A})\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})\sqrt{p/T})
, can be much larger than that in Theorem
6
, which confirms the efficacy of the proposed transformation method in removing the diverging effect. Finally, when
\delta_{s}>0
, i.e.,
\|\mathbf{A}^{*}\|_{\text{F}}
diverges with
p
, we may also consider the relative estimation error
\|\mathbf{A}^{(I)}-\mathbf{A}^{*}\|_{\text{F}}/\|\mathbf{A}^{*}\|_{\text{F}}\lesssim\tau^{2}\sqrt{p^{1+\delta_{u}-\delta_{v}-2\delta_{s}}/T}
for the estimation consistency of factor loadings.

6
Sparsity on Factor Loading Matrices

The convergence analysis in Sections
4
and
5
requires
T\gtrsim p
or
T\gtrsim p^{1+\delta_{u}-\delta_{v}}
; however, the number of series
p
could be comparable to or even larger than the sample size
T
for some real applications.
For this case, the common and specific factors are related to only a small subset of variables, while many other variables have no contribution in extracting factors.
Thus, in order to improve the estimation efficiency and model interpretation, it is of interest to further consider additional row-wise sparsity structure to factor loading matrices. If the factor loadings are sparse, it is unlikely to have strong cross-sectional dependence, so in this section, the diverging eigenvalue effect is not considered.

We consider the case of VAR(1) models and the result can be extended to that of general VAR(
\ell
) models. Suppose that there are at most
s_{c}
,
s_{r}
, and
s_{p}
variables related to the common, response-specific, and predictor-specific factors, respectively.
Let
\mathbb{S}(p,q,s)=\{\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}:\text{the number of non-zero rows of }\mathbf{M}\text{ is at most }s\}
. We can extend the regularized estimation method in (
3.2
) to encourage the row-wise sparsity on
\mathbf{C}
,
\mathbf{R}
, and
\mathbf{P}
,

\begin{split}\left(\widehat{\mathbf{C}},\widehat{\mathbf{R}},\widehat{\mathbf{P}},\widehat{\mathbf{D}}\right)=\argmin_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{S}(p,d,s_{c}),\mathbf{D}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r},\\
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{S}(p,r-d,s_{r}),\mathbf{P}\in\mathbb{S}(p,r-d,s_{p})\end{subarray}}&\Bigg\{\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{R},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{D})+\frac{a}{2}\left\|\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}^{\top}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{R}\textbf{]}-b^{2}\mathbf{I}_{r}\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\\
&\hskip 82.51299pt+\frac{a}{2}\left\|\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}^{\top}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}~\mathbf{P}\textbf{]}-b^{2}\mathbf{I}_{r}\right\|_{\text{F}}^{2}\Bigg\}.\end{split}

(6.1)

Accordingly, for the row-wise sparsity constraint, the hard thresholding operation
\text{HT}(\mathbf{M},s)
can be added to the gradient descent algorithm, where
\text{HT}(\mathbf{M},s)
projects the matrix
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
onto
\mathbb{S}(p,q,s)
by keeping the top
s
largest rows of
\mathbf{M}
in terms of Euclidean norm and truncating the rest to zeros; see Algorithm
2
.
Note that the row-wise sparsity structure is invariant with respect to rotation.

Algorithm 2

Hard thresholding gradient descent algorithm for sparse estimation

1:
Input
:
\mathbf{Y}
,
\mathbf{X}
,
\eta
,
I
,
\mathbf{C}^{(0)}
,
\mathbf{R}^{(0)}
,
\mathbf{P}^{(0)}
,
\mathbf{D}^{(0)}
, and sparsity level
(s_{c},s_{r},s_{p})
.

2:
for

i=0,\dots,I-1

3:    Use lines 3-6 in Algorithm
1
to obtain
\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{(i+1)}
,
\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}^{(i+1)}
,
\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}^{(i+1)}
, and
\mathbf{D}^{(i+1)}

4:
\mathbf{C}^{(i+1)}=\text{HT}(\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{(i+1)},s_{c})
,
\mathbf{R}^{(i+1)}=\text{HT}(\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}^{(i+1)},s_{r})
, and
\mathbf{P}^{(i+1)}=\text{HT}(\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}^{(i+1)},s_{p})

5:
end for

6:
Return
:
\mathbf{A}^{(I)}=\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}^{(I)}~\mathbf{R}^{(I)}\textbf{]}\mathbf{D}^{(I)}\textbf{[}\mathbf{C}^{(I)}~\mathbf{P}^{(I)}\textbf{]}^{\top}

For initialization of Algorithm
2
, we conduct the
L_{1}
regularized least squares estimation

\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{L_{1}}=\argmin\{(2T)^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda\|\mathbf{A}\|_{1}\}
.
Denote by
\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}
the first
r
left and right singular vectors of
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}_{L_{1}}
, respectively, and then the spectral initialization method in Section
3.2
can be used to obtain
(\widetilde{\mathbf{C}},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\mathbf{P}},\widetilde{\mathbf{D}})
. Finally we set
\mathbf{C}^{(0)}=\text{HT}(b\widetilde{\mathbf{C}},s_{c})
,
\mathbf{R}^{(0)}=\text{HT}(b\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},s_{r})
,
\mathbf{P}^{(0)}=\text{HT}(b\widetilde{\mathbf{P}},s_{p})
, and
\mathbf{D}^{(0)}=b^{-2}\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}
.
The rank and common dimension can similarly be selected by the proposed methods in Section
3.3
.
The sparsity level
(s_{c},s_{r},s_{p})
can be determined by the domain knowledge or estimated based on
(\widetilde{\mathbf{C}},\widetilde{\mathbf{R}},\widetilde{\mathbf{P}})
.

Assume that the numbers of nonzero rows in the ground truth
\mathbf{C}^{*}
,
\mathbf{R}^{*}
, and
\mathbf{P}^{*}
are
s_{c}^{*}
,
s_{r}^{*}
, and
s_{p}^{*}
, respectively, and the sparsity levels in Algorithm
2
satisfy that
s_{c}\geq(1+\gamma)s_{c}^{*}
,
s_{r}\geq(1+\gamma)s_{r}^{*}
, and
s_{p}\geq(1+\gamma)s_{p}^{*}
, for some constant
\gamma>0
.

Theorem 7
.

Let
s=\max(s_{c}+s_{r},s_{c}+s_{p})
and
S^{*}=(s_{c}^{*}+s_{r}^{*})(s_{c}^{*}+s_{p}^{*})
. Suppose that
T\gtrsim\max(\tau^{4},\tau^{2})M_{2}^{-2}\{sr+r^{2}+s\min(\log(p),\log(ep/s))+S^{*}\log(p)\}
,
\lambda\asymp\tau^{2}M_{1}\sqrt{S^{*}\log(p)/T}
,
